Does Momentum Change the Implicit Regularization on Separable Data?
Bohan Wang, Qi Meng, Huishuai Zhang, Ruoyu Sun, Wei Chen, Zhi-Ming Ma, Tie-Yan Liu

Abstract
The momentum acceleration technique is widely adopted in many optimization algorithms. However, there is no theoretical answer on how the momentum affects the generalization performance of the optimization algorithms. This paper studies this problem by analyzing the implicit regularization of momentum-based optimization. We prove that on the linear classification problem with separable data and exponential-tailed loss, gradient descent with momentum (GDM) converges to the L 2 L_{2} max-margin solution, which is the same as vanilla gradient descent. That means gradient descent with momentum acceleration still converges to a low-complexity model, which guarantees their generalization. We then analyze the stochastic and adaptive variants of GDM (i.e., SGDM and deterministic Adam) and show they also converge to the L 2 L_{2} max-margin solution. Technically, to overcome the difficulty of the error accumulation in analyzing the momentum, we construct new potential functions to analyze the gap between the model parameter and the max-margin solution. Numerical experiments are conducted and support our theoretical results.



1
Introduction

It is widely believed that the optimizers have the implicit regularization in terms of selecting output parameters among all the minima on the landscape
[
23
,
14
,
40
]
. Parallel to the analysis of
coordinate descent
(
[
28
,
36
]
),
[
30
]
shows that
gradient descent
would converge to the
L^{2}
max-margin solution for the linear classification task with exponential-tailed loss, which mirrors its good generalization property in practice. Since then, many efforts have been taken on analyzing the implicit regularization of various local-search optimizers, including stochastic gradient descent
[
21
]
, steepest descent
[
6
]
, AdaGrad
[
26
]
and optimizers for homogeneous neural networks
[
18
,
11
,
38
]
.

However, though the momentum acceleration technique is widely adopted in the optimization algorithms in both convex and non-convex learning tasks
[
33
,
37
,
34
]
, the understanding of how the momentum would affect the generalization performance of the optimization algorithms is still unclear, as the historical gradients in the momentum may significantly change the searching direction of the optimization dynamics. A natural question is:

Can we theoretically analyze the implicit regularization of momentum-based optimizers?

In this paper, we take the first step to analyze the convergence of momentum-based optimizers and unveil their implicit regularization. Our study starts from the classification problem with the linear model and exponential-tailed loss using Gradient Descent with Momentum (GDM) optimizer. Then the variants of GDM such as Stochastic Gradient Descent with Momentum (SGDM) and deterministic Adam are also analyzed.
We consider the optimizers with constant learning rate and constant momentum hyper-parameters, which are widely adopted in practice, e.g., the default setting in popular machine learning frameworks
[
24
]
and in experiments
[
41
]
. Our main results are summarized in Theorem
1
.

Theorem 1

(informal)
.

With linearly separable dataset
\bm{S}
, linear model and exponential-tailed loss:

•

For GDM with a constant learning rate, the parameter norm diverges to infinity, with its direction converging to the
L^{2}
max-margin solution. The same conclusion holds for SGDM with a constant learning rate.

•

For deterministic Adam with a constant learning rate and stochastic RMSProp (i.e., Adam without momentum) with a decaying learning rate, the same conclusion holds.

Table 1:
The algorithms investigated in this paper (GDM and Adam) along with algorithms (GD) already investigated in the existing literature. We also compare the learning rates required to obtain the characterization of implicit regularization. As for stochastic Adam with
\beta_{1}\neq 0
, we leave its implicit regularization as future work.

]

]

\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\right)

in this Work

Theorem
1
states that GDM and its variants converge to the
L^{2}
max-margin solution, which is the same as their without-momentum versions, indicating that momentum does not affect the convergent direction. Therefore, the good generalization behavior of the output parameters of these optimizers is well validated as the margin of a classifier is positively correlated with its generalization error
[
13
]
and is supported by existing experimental observations (e.g.,
[
30
,
20
,
38
]
).

Our contributions are significant in terms of the following aspects:

•

We establish the implicit regularization of the momentum-based optimizers, an open problem since the initial work
[
30
]
. The momentum-based optimizers are widely used in practice, and our theoretical characterization deepens the understanding of their generalization property, which is important on its own.

•

Technically, we design a two-stage framework to analyze the momentum-based optimizers, which generalizes the proof techniques in
[
30
]
and
[
21
]
. The first stage shows the convergence of the loss. New potential functions for SGDM and Adam are proposed and can be of independent interest for convergence analysis of momentum-based optimizers. The second stage shows the convergence of the parameter. We propose an easy-to-check condition of whether the difference between learned parameters and the scaled max-margin solution is bounded. This condition can be generalized to implicit regularization analyses of other momentum-based optimizers.

•

We further verify our theory through numerical experiments.

Organization of This Paper.

Section
2
collects further related works on the implicit regularization of the first order optimizers and the convergence of momentum-based optimizers. Section
3
shows basic settings and assumptions which will be used throughout this paper. Section
4
studies the implicit regularization of GDM, while Section
5
and Section
6
explore respectively the implicit regularization of SGDM and Adam. Discussions of these results are put in Section
7
. Detailed proofs and experiments can be found in the appendix.

2
Further related works

Implicit Regularization of First-order Optimization Methods.

[
30
]
prove that gradient descent on linear classification problem with exponential-tailed loss converges to the direction of the max
L^{2}
margin solution of the corresponding hard-margin Support Vector Machine.
[
21
]
extend the results in
[
30
]
to the stochastic case, proving that the convergent direction of SGD is the same as GD almost surely.
[
26
]
go beyond the vanilla gradient descent methods and consider the AdaGrad optimizer instead. They prove that the convergent direction of AdaGrad has a dependency on the optimizing trajectory, which varies according to the initialization.
[
12
]
propose a primal-dual analysis framework for the linear classification models and prove a faster convergent rate of the margin by increasing the learning rate according to the loss. Based on
[
12
]
,
[
9
]
design another algorithm with an even faster convergent rate of margin by applying the Nesterov’s Acceleration Method on the dual space. However, the corresponding form of the algorithm on the primal space is no longer a Nesterov’s Acceleration Method nor GDM, which is significantly different from our settings.

On the other hand, another line of work is trying to extend the linear case result to deep neural networks.
[
10
,
7
]
study the deep linear network and
[
30
]
study the two-layer neural network with ReLU activation.
[
18
]
propose a framework to analyze the asymptotic direction of GD on homogeneous neural networks, proving that given there exists a time the network achieves
100\%
training accuracy, GD will converge to some KKT point of the
L^{2}
max-margin problem.
[
38
]
extend the framework of
[
18
]
to adaptive optimizers and prove RMSProp and Adam without momentum have the same convergent direction as GD, while AdaGrad does not. The results
[
18
,
38
]
indicate that results in the linear model can be extended to deep homogeneous neural networks and suggest that the linear model is an appropriate starting point to study the implicit bias.

Except for the exponential-tailed loss, there are also works on the implicit bias with squared loss. Interesting readers can refer to
[
27
,
16
,
1
]
etc. for details.

Convergence of Momentum-Based Optimization Methods.
For convex optimization problems, the convergence rate of Nesterov’s Acceleration Method
[
22
]
has been proved in various approaches (e.g.,
[
22
,
31
,
39
]
). In contrast, although GDM (Polyak’s Heavy-Ball Method) was proposed in
[
25
]
before the Nesterov’s Acceleration Method, the convergence of GDM on convex loss with Lipschitz gradient was not solved until
[
5
]
provides an ergodic convergent result for GDM, i.e., the convergent result for the running average of the iterates.
[
32
]
provide a non-ergodic analysis when the training loss is coercive (the training loss goes to infinity whenever parameter norm goes to infinity), convex, and globally smooth. However, all existing results cannot be directly applied to exponential-tailed loss, which is non-coercive.

There are also works on the convergence of SGDM under various settings.
[
42
]
prove SGDM converges to a bounded region assuming both bounded gradient norm and bounded gradient variance. The bounded gradient norm assumption is further removed by
[
43
,
17
]
. Nevertheless, a converging-to-stationary-point analysis is required in the implicit regularization analysis. Thus their results can not be directly applied.
[
35
]
analyze a particular case when the momentum parameter increases over iterations, which, however, does not agree with the practice where the momentum parameter is fixed.

As for (stochastic) Adam, its convergence analysis is still an open problem, and the current analyses are restricted to specific settings (e.g., bounded gradient, dynamical momentum hyperparameters). We recommend interested readers to refer to
[
4
,
3
,
29
,
8
]
for details.

3
Preliminaries

This paper focuses on the linear model with the exponential-tailed loss. We mainly investigate binary classification. However, the methodology can be easily extended to the multi-class classification problem (please refer to Appendix
E.3
for details).

Problem setting.
The dataset used for training is denoted as
\bm{S}=(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
, where
\bm{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the
i
-th input feature, and
\bm{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
is the
i
-th label (
i=1,2,\cdots,N
). We will use the linear model to fit the label: for any feature
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and parameter
\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the prediction is given by
\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle
.

For binary classification, given any data
\bm{z}_{i}=(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{y}_{i})\in\bm{S}
, the individual loss for parameter
\bm{w}
is given as
\ell(\bm{y}_{i}\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)
. As only
\bm{y}_{i}\bm{x}_{i}
is used in the loss, we then ensemble the feature and label together and assume
\bm{y}_{i}=1
(
\forall i\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
) without the loss of generality. We then drop
\bm{y}_{i}
for brevity and redefine
\bm{S}=(\bm{x}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
. The spectral norm of the data matrix
(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{N})
is defined as
\sigma_{max}
.
We use

\tilde{\ell}(\bm{w},\bm{x})\triangleq\ell(\langle\bm{w},{\bm{x}}\rangle)
for brevity.

The optimization target is defined as the averaged loss:

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\ell}(\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i})}{N}.

Optimizer.
Here we will introduce the update rules of GDM, SGDM and deterministic Adam. GDM’s update rule is

\bm{m}(0)=\bm{0},\bm{m}(t)=\beta\bm{m}(t-1)+(1-\beta)\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t)),\bm{w}(t+1)=\bm{w}(t)-\eta\bm{m}(t).

(1)

SGDM can be viewed as a stochastic version of GDM by randomly choosing a subset of the dataset to update. Specifically, SGDM changes the update of
\bm{m}(t)
into

\bm{m}(t)=\beta\bm{m}(t-1)+(1-\beta)\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(t)}(\bm{w}(t)),

(2)

where
\bm{B}(t)
is a subset of
\bm{S}
with size
b
which can be sampled either with replacement (
abbreviated as "w/. r"
) or without replacement (i.e., with random shuffling,
abbreviated as "w/o. r"
), and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(t)}
is defined as

{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(t)}(\bm{w})=\frac{\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{B}(t)}\tilde{\ell}(\bm{w},\bm{x})}{b}
. We also define
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
as the sub-sigma algebra over the mini-batch sampling, such that
\forall t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\bm{w}(t)
is adapted with respect to the sigma algebra flow
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
.

The Adam optimizer can be viewed as a variant of SGDM in which the preconditioner is adopted, whose form is characterized as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{m}(0)=\bm{0},\bm{m}(t)=\beta_{1}\bm{m}(t-1)+(1-\beta_{1})\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(t)}(\bm{w}(t)),

\displaystyle\bm{\nu}(0)=0,\bm{\nu}(t)=\beta_{2}\bm{\nu}(t-1)+(1-\beta_{2})\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\odot\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{m}}(t)=\frac{1}{1-\beta^{t}_{1}}\bm{m}(t),\hat{\bm{\nu}}(t)=\frac{1}{1-\beta^{t}_{2}}\bm{\nu}(t),

\displaystyle\textit{(Update Rule)}:~\bm{w}(t)=\bm{w}(t-1)-\eta\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{\bm{\nu}}(t-1)+\varepsilon\mathds{1}_{d}}}\odot\hat{\bm{m}}(t-1),

(3)

where
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{\bm{\nu}}(t-1)+\varepsilon\mathds{1}_{d}}}
is called the preconditioner.

Assumptions:
The analysis of this paper are based on three common assumptions in existing literature (first proposed by
[
30
]
). They are respectively on the separability of the dataset, the individual loss behavior at the tail, and the smoothness of the individual loss. We list them as follows:

Assumption 1

(Linearly Separable Dataset)
.

There exists one parameter
\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that

\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0,~\forall i\in[N].

Assumption 2

(Exponential-tailed Loss)
.

The individual loss
\ell
is exponential-tailed, i.e.,

•

Differentiable and monotonically decreasing to zero, with its derivative converging to zero at positive infinity and to non-zero at negative infinity, i.e.,
\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\ell(x)

=\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\ell^{\prime}(x)=0
,
\varlimsup_{x\rightarrow-\infty}\ell^{\prime}(x)<0
, and
\ell^{\prime}(x){<}0
,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}
;

•

Close to exponential loss when
x
is large enough, i.e., there exist positive constants
c,a,\mu_{+},\mu_{-},x_{+}
, and
x_{-}
, such that,

\displaystyle\forall x>x_{+}:-\ell^{\prime}(x)\leq c(1+e^{-\mu_{+}x})e^{-ax},

(4)

\displaystyle\forall x>x_{-}:-\ell^{\prime}(x)\geq c(1-e^{-\mu_{-}x})e^{-ax}.

(5)

Assumption 3

(Smooth Loss)
.

Either of the following assumptions holds regarding the case:

(D):
(Without Stochasticity) The individual loss
\ell
is locally smooth, i.e., for any
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a positive real
H_{s_{0}}
, such that
\forall x,y\geq s_{0}
,
|\ell^{\prime}(x)-\ell^{\prime}(y)|\leq H_{s_{0}}|x-y|
.

(S):
(With Stochasticity) The individual loss
\ell
is globally smooth, i.e., there exists a positive real
H
, such that
\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,
|\ell^{\prime}(x)-\ell^{\prime}(y)|\leq H|x-y|
.

We provide explanations of these three assumptions, respectively. Based on Assumption
1
, we can formally define the margin and the maximum margin solution of an optimization problem:

Definition 1
.

Let the margin
\hat{\gamma}(\bm{w})
of parameter
\bm{w}
defined as the lowest score of the prediction of
\bm{w}
over the dataset
\bm{S}
, i.e.,
\hat{\gamma}(\bm{w})=\min_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle
. We then define the maximum margin solution
\hat{\bm{w}}
and the
L^{2}
max margin
\gamma
of the dataset S as follows:

\hat{\bm{w}}\overset{\triangle}{=}\arg\min_{\hat{\gamma}(\bm{w})\geq 1}\|\bm{w}\|^{2},~\gamma\overset{\triangle}{=}\frac{1}{\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|}

Since
\|\cdot\|^{2}
is strongly convex and set
\{\bm{w}:\hat{\gamma}(\bm{w})\geq 1\}
is convex,
\hat{\bm{w}}
is uniquely defined.

Assumption
2
constraints the loss to be exponential-tailed, which is satisfied by many popular choices of
\ell
, including the exponential loss (
\ell_{exp}(x)=e^{-x}
) and the logistic loss (
\ell_{log}(x)=\log(1+e^{-x})
).
Also, as
c
and
a
can be respectively absorbed by resetting the learning rate and data as
\eta=c\eta
and
\bm{x}_{i}=a\bm{x}_{i}
, without loss of generality,
in this paper we only analyze the case that
c=a=1
.

The globally smooth assumption (Assumption
3
. (S)) is strictly stronger than the locally smooth assumption (Assumption
3
. (D)). One can easily verify that both the exponential loss and the logistic loss meet Assumption
3
. (
D
), and the logistic loss also meets Assumption
3
. (
S
).

4
The implicit regularization of GDM

In this section, we analyze the implicit regularization of GDM with a two-stage framework
1
1

1

It should be noticed that the proof sketches in Sections
4
,
5
, and
6
only hold for almost every dataset (means except a zero-measure set in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}
), as we want the presentation more simple and straightforward. However, the proof can be extended to every dataset with a more careful analysis (please refer to Appendix
E.1
for details).
. Later, we will use this framework to investigate SGDM further and deterministic Adam. The formal theorem of the implicit regularization of GDM is as follows:

Theorem 2
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
. (
D
) hold. Let
\beta\in[0,1)
and
\eta<2\frac{N}{\sigma_{max}^{2}H_{\ell^{-1}}(N\mathcal{L}(\bm{w}_{1}))}
(
\ell^{-1}
is the inverse function of
\ell
). Then, for almost every data set
S
, with arbitrary initialization point
\bm{w}(1)
, GDM (Eq. equation
1
) satisfies that

\bm{w}(t)-\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}
is bounded as
t\rightarrow\infty
, and
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\bm{w}(t)}{\|\bm{w}(t)\|}=\frac{\hat{\bm{w}}}{\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|}
.

Theorem
2
shows that the implicit regularization of GDM agrees with GD in linear classification with exponential-tailed loss (c.f.
[
30
]
for results on GD). This consistency can be verified by existing and our experiments (c.f. Section
7
for detailed discussions).

Remark 1

(On the hyperparameter setting)
.

Firstly, the learning rate upper bound
2\frac{N}{\sigma_{max}^{2}H_{\ell^{-1}}(N\mathcal{L}(\bm{w}_{1}))}
agrees with that of GD exactly
[
30
]
, indicating our analysis is tight.
Secondly, Theorem
2
adopts a constant momentum hyper-parameter, which agrees with the practical use (e.g.,
\beta
is fixed to be
0.9

[
41
]
). Also, Theorem
2
puts no restriction on the range of
\beta
, which allows wider choices of hyper-parameter tuning.

We then present a proof sketch of Theorem
2
, which is divided into two parts: we first prove that the sum of squared gradients is bounded, which indicates both the loss and the norm of gradient converge to
0
and the parameter diverges to infinity; these properties will then be applied to show the difference between
\bm{w}(t)
and
\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}
is bounded, and therefore, the direction of
\hat{\bm{w}}
dominates as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

Stage I: Loss Dynamics.
The goal of this stage is to characterize the dynamics of the loss and prove the convergence of GDM. The core of this stage is to select a proper potential function
\xi(t)
, which is required to correlate with the training loss
{\mathcal{L}}
and be non-increasing along the optimization trajectory. For GD, since
{\mathcal{L}}
is non-increasing with a properly chosen learning rate, we can pick
\xi(t)={\mathcal{L}}(t)
. However, as the update of GDM does not align with the direction of the negative gradient, training loss
{\mathcal{L}}(t)
in GDM is no longer monotonously decreasing, and the potential function requires special construction. Inspired by
[
32
]
, we choose the following
\xi(t)
:

Lemma 1
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
2
hold. Define
\xi(t)\overset{\triangle}{=}{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))+\frac{1-\beta}{2\beta\eta}\|\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\|^{2}.
Define
{C_{1}}
as a positive real with
{C_{1}}\triangleq\frac{\sigma_{max}^{2}H_{\ell^{-1}}(N\mathcal{L}(\bm{w}_{1}))}{2N}\eta
. We then have

\displaystyle\xi(t)\geq\xi(t+1)+\frac{1-{C_{1}}}{\eta}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}.

(6)

Remark 2
.

Although this potential function is obtained by
[
32
]
by directly examining Taylor’s expansion at
\bm{w}(t)
, the proof here is non-trivial as we only require the loss to be locally smooth instead of globally smooth in
[
32
]
. We need to prove that the smoothness parameter along the trajectory is upper bounded. We defer the detailed proof to Appendix
B.1.1
.

By Lemma
1
, we have that
\xi(t)
is monotonously decreasing by gap
\frac{1-C_{1}}{\eta}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}
. As
\xi(1)={\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))
is a finite number, we have
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}<\infty
. By that
(1-\beta)\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))=(\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t))-\beta(\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1))
, it immediately follows that
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}

\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\|^{2}<\infty
.

Stage II. Parameter Dynamics.
The goal of this stage is to characterize the dynamics of the parameter and show that GDM asymptotically converges (in direction) to the max-margin solution
\hat{\bm{w}}
. To see this, we define a residual term
\bm{r}(t)\overset{\triangle}{=}\bm{w}(t)-\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}-\tilde{\bm{w}}
with some constant vector
\tilde{\bm{w}}
(specified in Appendix
B.1.2
). If we can show the norm of
\bm{r}(t)
is bounded over the iterations, we complete the proof as
\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}
will then dominates the dynamics of
\bm{w}(t)
.

For simplicity, we use the continuous dynamics approximation of GDM
[
32
]
to demonstrate why
\bm{r}(t)
is bounded:

\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\bm{w}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}+\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(t)}{\mathrm{d}t}+\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))=0.

(7)

We start by directly examining the evolution of
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(T)\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(1)\|^{2}=\int_{1}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\|\bm{r}(s)\|^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{T}\left\langle\bm{r}(s),-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s))-\frac{1}{s}\hat{\bm{w}}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s+\int_{1}^{T}\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\left\langle\bm{r}(s),-\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\bm{w}(s)}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s,

which by integration by part leads to

\operatorname{RHS}=\int_{1}^{T}\left\langle\bm{r}(s),-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s))-\frac{1}{s}\hat{\bm{w}}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\left(\left\langle\bm{r}(T),-\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(T)}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\rangle-\left\langle\bm{r}(1),-\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(1)}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\rangle+\int_{1}^{T}\left\langle\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{r}(s)}{\mathrm{d}s},\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(s)}{\mathrm{d}s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s\right),

We then check the terms one by one:

•

\int_{1}^{T}\left\langle\bm{r}(s),-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s))-\frac{1}{s}\hat{\bm{w}}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s
: This term also occurs in the analysis of GD
[
30
]
, and has been proved to be bounded;

•

\langle\bm{r}(T),-\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(T)}{\mathrm{d}t}\rangle
: as shown in Stage I,
\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(T)}{\mathrm{d}t}\rightarrow 0
(i.e.,
\bm{w}(T)-\bm{w}(T-1)

\rightarrow 0
in the discrete case) as
T\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, this term is
\bm{o}(\|\bm{r}(T)\|)
;

•

\int_{1}^{T}\left\langle\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{r}(s)}{\mathrm{d}s},\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(s)}{\mathrm{d}s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s
: finite due to
\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{r}(s)}{\mathrm{d}s}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(s)}{\mathrm{d}s}-\frac{1}{s}\hat{\bm{w}}
and
\int_{1}^{\infty}\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{w}(s)}{\mathrm{d}s}\|^{2}\mathrm{d}s
is finite (i.e.,
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}

\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}<\infty
in the discrete case) by Stage I.

Putting them together, we show that
\|\bm{r}(T)\|^{2}+\bm{o}(\|\bm{r}(T)\|)
is upper bounded over the iterations, which immediately leads to that
\|\bm{r}(T)\|
is bounded. Applying similar methodology to the discrete update rule, we have the following lemma (the proof can be found in Appendix
B.1.2
).

Lemma 2
.

Define potential function
g:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle g(t)\overset{\triangle}{=}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\rangle.

g(t)
is upper bounded, which further indicates
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
is upper bounded.

Remark 3
.

Our technique for analyzing GDM here is essentially more complex and elaborate than that for GD in
[
30
]
due to the historical information of gradients GDM. The approach in
[
30
]
cannot be directly applied. It is worth mentioning that we provide a more easy-to-check condition for whether
\bm{r}(t)
is bounded, i.e., "is
g(t)
upper-bounded?". This condition can be generalized for other momentum-based implicit regularization analyses. E.g., SGDM and Adam later in this paper.

5
Tackle the difficulty brought by random sampling

In this section, we analyze the implicit regularization of SGDM. Parallel to GDM, we establish the following implicit regularization result for SGDM:

Theorem 3
.

Let Assumption
1
,
2
, and
3
. (
S
) hold. Let
\beta\in[0,1)
and
\eta<\frac{1}{\frac{H\beta\sigma_{max}^{3}}{\sqrt{Nb}\gamma(1-\beta)}+\frac{H\sigma_{max}^{4}}{2b\gamma^{2}}}
. Then, with arbitrary initialization point
\bm{w}(1)
, SGDM (w/. r) satisfies

\bm{w}(t)-\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}
is bounded as
t\rightarrow\infty
and
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\bm{w}(t)}{\|\bm{w}(t)\|}=\frac{\hat{\bm{w}}}{\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|}
, almost surely (a.s.).

Similar to the GDM case, Theorem
3
shows that the implicit regularization of SGDM under this setting is consistent with SGD (c.f.
[
21
]
for the implicit regularization of SGD). This matches the observations in practice (c.f. Section
7
for details), and is later supported by our experiments (e.g., Figure
1
). We add two remarks on the learning rate upper bound and extension to SGDM (w/. r).

Remark 4

(On the learning rate)
.

Firstly, our learning rate upper bound
\frac{1}{\frac{H\beta\sigma_{max}^{3}}{\sqrt{Nb}\gamma(1-\beta)}+\frac{H\sigma_{max}^{4}}{2b\gamma^{2}}}
exactly matches that of SGD
2\frac{b\gamma^{2}}{H\sigma^{4}_{max}}

[
21
]
when
\beta=0
, and matches that of SGD in terms of the order of
\sigma_{max}
,
H
, and
b
when
\beta\neq 0
. This indicates our analysis is tight. Secondly, as the bound is monotonously increasing with respect to batch size
b
, Theorem
3
also sheds light on the learning rate tuning, i.e., the larger the batch size is, the larger the learning rate is.

Remark 5
.

(On SGDM (w/o. r))

Theorem
3
can be similarly extended to SGDM (w/o. r). We defer the detailed description of the corresponding theorem together with the proof to Appendix
E.2
.

Next, we show the proof sketch for Theorem 3. The proof also contains two stages, where Stage II is similar to that for GDM. However, we highlight that
Stage I for SGDM is not a trivial extension of that for GDM and has its merit for other optimization analyses for SGDM
. Specifically, the methodology used to construct GDM’s potential function fails for SGDM due to the random sampling. We defer a detailed discussion to Appendix
B.2.3
. We then need to find a proper potential function for SGDM.
Inspired by SGD’s simple update rule, we rearrange the update rule of SGDM such that only the gradient information of the current step is contained, i.e.,

\frac{\bm{w}(t+1)-\beta\bm{w}(t)}{1-\beta}=\frac{\bm{w}(t)-\beta\bm{w}(t-1)}{1-\beta}-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(t)}(\bm{w}(t)).

By defining
\bm{u}(t)\triangleq\frac{\bm{w}(t)-\beta\bm{w}(t-1)}{1-\beta}=\bm{w}(t)+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}(\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1))
, we have that
\bm{u}(t)
is close to
\bm{w}(t)
(differs by order of one-step update
\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)
), and the update rule of
\bm{u}(t)
only contains the current-step-gradient information
\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))
. We then select potential function as
{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(t))
, and a simple Taylor’s expansion directly leads to:

{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(t+1))|{\mathcal{F}}_{t}]\approx{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(t))-\eta\langle\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t)),\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(t))\rangle\approx{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(t))-\eta\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\|^{2},

i.e.,
{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(t))
is a proper potential function. We formalize the above discussion into the following lemma.

Lemma 3
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
3
hold. Then, there exists a positive constant
C_{2}
, such that

{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(t+1))]\leq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(1))-\sum_{s=1}^{t}C_{2}\eta{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s))\|^{2}.

By letting
T\rightarrow\infty
in the second claim of Lemma
3
, we have
\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s))\|^{2}<\infty
, which is indeed what we need in Stage I.

6
Analyze the effect of preconditioners

6.1
Implicit regularization of deterministic Adam

This section presents the implicit regularization of deterministic Adam, i.e., Adam without random sampling.

Theorem 4
.

Let Assumption
1
,
2
, and
3
. (
D
) hold. Let
1>\beta_{2}>\beta_{1}^{4}\geq 0
, and the learning rate
\eta
is a small enough constant (The upper bound of learning rate is complex, and we defer it to Appendix
C.1
). Then, with arbitrary initialization point
\bm{w}(1)
, deterministic Adam (Eq.
3
) satisfies that

\bm{w}(t)-\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}
is bounded as
t\rightarrow\infty
, and
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\bm{w}(t)}{\|\bm{w}(t)\|}=\frac{\hat{\bm{w}}}{\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|}
.

Remark 6

(On the
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
range)
.

Almost all existing literature assume a time-decaying hyperparameter choice of
\beta_{1}
or
\beta_{2}
(c.f.,
[
15
,
2
]
). On the other hand, our result proves that deterministic Adam converges with constant settings of
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
, which agrees with the practical use.

Remark 7

((Discussion on the results in
[
30
]
))
.

[
30
]
observe that, on a synthetic dataset, the direction of the output parameter by Adam still does not converge to the max-margin direction after
2\times 10^{6}
iterations (but is getting closer). At the same time, GD seems to converge to the max-margin direction. This seems to contradict with Theorem
4
. We reconduct the experiments (please refer to Appendix
F.1.2
for details) and found (1). such a phenomenon occurs because a large learning rate is selected, using which GD will also stick in a direction close to the max-margin direction (but not equal); (2). the constructed synthetic dataset is ill-posed, as the non-support data is larger than the support data by order of magnitude (which is also rare in practice). On a well-posed dataset, we observe that both Adam and GD will converge to the max-margin solution rapidly (Figure
1
).

We simply introduce the proof idea here and put the full proof in Appendix
C
. The proof of deterministic Adam is very similar to that of GDM with minor changes in potential functions. Specifically,
\xi(t)
in Lemma
1
is changed to

\xi(t)\triangleq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))+\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\beta_{1}^{t-1}}{\eta(1-\beta_{1})}\left\|\sqrt[4]{\varepsilon\mathds{1}_{d}+\hat{\bm{\nu}}(t-1)}\odot(\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1))\right\|^{2},

and
g(t)
in Lemma
2
is changed to

\displaystyle g(t)\overset{\triangle}{=}\left\langle\bm{r}(t),(1-\beta_{1}^{t-1})\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathds{1}_{d}+\hat{\bm{\nu}}(t-1)}\odot\left(\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\right)\right\rangle\frac{\beta_{1}}{1-\beta_{1}}+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2}\|\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}.

The rest of the proof then flows similarly to the GDM case.

6.2
What if random sampling is added?

We have obtained the implicit regularization for GDM, SGDM, and deterministic Adam. One may wonder whether the implicit regularization of stochastic Adam can be obtained. Unfortunately, the gap can not be closed yet. This is because an implicit regularization analysis requires the knowledge of the loss dynamics, little of which, however, has been ever known even for stochastic RMSProp (i.e., Adam with
\beta_{1}=0
in Eq. (
3
)) with constant learning rates. Specifically, the main difficulty lies in bounding the change of conditioner
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathds{1}_{d}+\hat{\bm{\nu}}(t)}}
across iterations, which is required to make the drift term
\langle\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t)),{\mathbb{E}}(\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t))\rangle
(derived by Taylor’s expansion of the epoch start from
Kt
) negative to ensure a non-increasing loss.

On the other hand, if we adopt decaying learning rates
\eta_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}
,
[
29
]
shows
\beta_{2}
close enough to
1
, the following equation holds for stochastic RMSProp (w/o. r) (recall that
K\triangleq\frac{N}{b}
is the epoch size)

\sum_{t=0}^{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t+1}}\left\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}\left(\bm{w}\left(Kt\right)\right)\right\|=\mathcal{O}(\ln T).

(8)

Based on this result, we have the following theorem for stochastic RMSProp (w/o. r):

Theorem 5
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
. (S) hold. Let
\beta_{2}
be close enough to
1
.
Then, with arbitrary initialization point

\bm{w}(1)
, stochastic RMSProp (w/o. r) converges to the
L^{2}
max-margin solution.

Remark 8

(On the decaying learning rate)
.

The decaying learning rate is a "stronger" setting compared to the constant learning rate, both in the sense that GDM, SGDM, and deterministic Adam can be shown to converge to the max-margin solution following the same routine as Theorems
2
,
3
, and
4
, and in the sense that we usually adopt constant learning rate in practice.

The proof is on the grounds of a novel characterization of the loss convergence rate derived from Eq. (
8
), and readers can find the details in Appendix
D
.
Furthermore, the proof can be easily extended to the Stochastic Adaptive Heavy Ball (SAHB) algorithm (proposed by
[
35
]
), which can be viewed as a momentum version of RMSProp (but different from Adam) with the following update rule

\bm{w}(t+1)=\bm{w}(t)-\bm{m}(t),\bm{m}(t)=\eta_{t}(1-\beta_{1})\frac{\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(t)}(\bm{w}(t))}{\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathds{1}_{d}+\hat{\bm{\nu}}(t)}}+\beta_{1}(\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)).

The proof of Theorem
5
can be easily extended to SAHB, based on the fact that
\bm{u}(t)=\frac{\bm{w}(t)-\beta_{1}\bm{w}(t-1)}{1-\beta_{1}}
has a simple update, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bm{u}(t+1)=-\eta_{t}\frac{\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(t)}(\bm{w}(t))}{\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathds{1}_{d}+\hat{\bm{\nu}}(t)}}+\bm{u}(t).

and
{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{u}(t))
can be used as a potential function just as the SGDM case.

Figure 1:
Comparison of the implicit regularization of (S)GD, (S)GDM, deterministic Adam and stochastic RMSProp. We use the synthetic dataset in
[
30
]
with learning rate
0.1
. Figure (b) shows (1). all the optimizers converge to the max margin solution, and (2). the asymptotic behaviors with & without momentum are similar. The experimental observation support our theoretical results.

(a)
Training Accuracy

(b)
Angle gap between the parameter direction and the max margin solution

7
Discussions

Consistency with the Experimental Results.
We conduct experiments to verify our theoretical findings. Specifically, we (1). run GD, GDM, SGD, SGDM, and Adam on a synthetic dataset to observe their implicit regularization; (2). run GD and Adam on ill-posed dataset proposed in
[
30
]
to verify Theorem
4
; (3). run SGD and SGDM on neural networks to classify the MNIST dataset and compare their implicit regularization. The experimental observations stand with our theoretical results. Furthermore, it is worth mentioning that experimental phenomenons that adding momentum will not change the implicit regularization have also been observed by existing literature
[
30
,
20
,
38
]
.

Influence of hyperparameters on convergence rates.
Our results can be further extended to provide a precise characterization of the influence of the hyperparameters
\eta
and
\beta
on the convergence rate of (S)GDM. Specifically, in Appendix
E.4
, we show that the asymptotic convergence rate of (S)GDM is
C\frac{1}{\eta}\frac{1}{t}
, where
C
is some constant independent of
\beta
and
\eta
. Therefore, increase
\eta
can lead to a faster convergence rate (with learning rate requirements in Theorems
2
and
3
satisfied). However, changing
\beta
does not affect the convergence rate, which is also observed in our experiments (e.g., Figure
1
). While this seems weird, as replacing gradient with momentum in deep learning often accelerates the training, we hypothesize that such an acceleration appears as the landscape of neural networks is highly non-convex and thus can not be observed in the case considered by this paper.

Gap Between The Linear Model and Deep Neural Networks.
While our results only hold for the linear classification problem, extending the results to the deep neural networks is possible. Specifically, existing literature
[
18
,
38
]
provide a framework for deriving implicit regularization for deep homogeneous neural networks. However, the approach in
[
18
,
38
]
can not be trivially applied to the momentum-based optimizers, as their proofs require the specific gradient-based updates to lower bound a smoothed margin (c.f., Theorem 4.1,
[
18
]
). It remains an exciting work to see how our results can be expanded to GDM
and Adam for deep neural networks.

8
Conclusion

This paper studies the implicit regularization of momentum-based optimizers in linear classification with exponential-tailed loss. Our results indicate that for SGD and the deterministic version of Adam, adding momentum will not influence the implicit regularization, and the direction of the parameter converges to the
L^{2}
max-margin solution. Our theoretical results stand with existing experimental observations, and developed techniques such as the potential functions may inspire the analyses on other momentum-based optimizers. Motivated by the results and techniques for linear cases in this paper, it has the potential to extend them to the homogeneous neural network in the future. Another topic left for future work is to derive the implicit regularization of constant learning rate stochastic Adam. As discussed in Section
6.2
, this topic is non-trivial, and it requires new techniques and assumptions to be developed.

Supplementary Materials for

“ Does Momentum Change the Implicit Regularization on Separable Data?”

Appendix A
Preparations

This section collect definitions and lemmas which will be used throughout the proofs.

A.1
Characterization of the max-margin solution

This section collects several commonly-used characterization of the max-margin solution from
[
21
]
and
[
30
]
.

To start with, we define support vectors and support set, which are two common terms in margin analysis. Recall that in the main text, we assume that without the loss of generality,
\bm{y}_{i}=1
,
\forall i\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
.

Definition 2

(Support vectors and support set)
.

For any
i\in[N]
,
\bm{x}_{i}
is called a support vector of the dataset
\bm{S}
, if

\langle\bm{x}_{i},\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle=1.

Correspondingly,
\bm{x}_{i}
is called a non-support vector if
\langle\bm{x}_{i},\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle>1
. The support set of
\bm{S}
is then defined as

\bm{S}_{s}=\{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}:\langle\bm{x},\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle=1\}.

The following lemma delivers
\hat{\bm{w}}
as an linear combination of support vectors.

Lemma 4

(Lemma 12,
[
30
]
)
.

For almost every datasets
S
, there exists a unique vector
\bm{v}=(\bm{v}_{1},\cdots,\bm{v}_{N})
, such that
\hat{\bm{w}}
can be represented as

\hat{\bm{w}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bm{v}_{i}\bm{x}_{i},

(9)

where
\bm{v}
satisfies
\bm{v}_{i}=0
if
\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s}
, and
\bm{v}_{i}>0
if
\bm{x}_{i}\in\bm{S}_{s}
. Furthermore, the size of
\tilde{\bm{S}}_{s}
is at most
d
.

By Lemma
4
, we further have the following corollary:

Corollary 1
.

For almost every datasets
S
, the unique
\bm{v}
given by Lemma
4
further satisfies that for any positive
constant
{C_{3}}
, there exists a non-zero vector
\tilde{\bm{w}}
, such that,
\bm{x}_{i}\in\bm{S}_{s}
, we have

{C_{3}}e^{-\langle\bm{x}_{i},\tilde{\bm{w}}\rangle}=\bm{v}_{i}.

(10)

Proof.

For almost every datasets
\bm{S}
, any subsets with size
d
of
\bm{S}
is linearly independent. Since
\tilde{\bm{S}}_{s}
has size no larger than
d
(by Lemma
4
), and Eq. (
10
) is equivalent to linear equations, the proof is completed.
∎

For the stochastic case, we will also need the following lemma when we calculate the form of parameter at time
t
.

Lemma 5

(Lemma 5,
[
21
]
)
.

Let
\bm{B}(s)
be the random subset used in SGDM (w/. r). Almost surely, there exists a vector
\check{\bm{w}}

\frac{N}{b}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\frac{1}{s}\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\in\bm{B}(s)\cap\bm{S}_{s}}\bm{v}_{i}\bm{x}_{i}=\ln\left(\frac{bt}{N}\right)\hat{\bm{w}}+\bm{n}(t)+\check{\bm{w}},

where
\bm{n}(t)
satisfies
\|\bm{n}(t)\|=\bm{o}(t^{-0.5+\varepsilon})
for any
\varepsilon>0
, and
\|\bm{n}(t+1)-\bm{n}(t)\|=O(t^{-1})
. As for SGDM (w/o. r), the a.s. condition can be removed.

A.2
Preparations of the optimization analysis

This section collects technical lemmas which will be used in latter proofs. We begin with a lemma bounding the smooth constants if the loss is bounded.

Lemma 6
.

If loss
\ell
satisfies (D) in Assumption
3
, then for any
\bm{w}_{0}
, if
{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\leq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{0})
, then we have
{\mathcal{L}}
is
\sigma_{max}^{2}H_{s_{0}}
smooth at point
\bm{w}
, where
s_{0}=\ell^{-1}(N{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{0}))
. Furthermore,
{\mathcal{L}}
is globally
\sigma_{max}^{2}H_{s_{0}}
smooth over the set
\{\bm{w}:{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\leq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{0})\}
.

Proof.

Since
\ell
is positive, we have
\forall i\in[N]
,

\frac{\tilde{\ell}(\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i})}{N}<\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tilde{\ell}(\bm{w},\bm{x}_{j})}{N}={\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\leq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{0}),

which leads to
\tilde{\ell}(\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i})<N{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{0})
, and
\ell
is
H_{s_{0}}
smooth at
\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle
.

Furthermore, since
\nabla_{\bm{w}}\tilde{\ell}(\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i})=\nabla_{\bm{w}}\ell(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)=\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}
, for any two parameters
\bm{w}_{1}
and
\bm{w}_{2}
close enough to
\bm{w}
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\bm{w}}{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{1})-\nabla_{\bm{w}}{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{2})\|=\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}(\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}_{1},\bm{x}\rangle)-\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}_{2},\bm{x}\rangle))\bm{x}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma_{max}\sqrt{\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}(\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}_{1},\bm{x}\rangle)-\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}_{2},\bm{x}\rangle))^{2}}\leq\sigma_{max}H_{s_{0}}\sqrt{\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}(\langle\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2},\bm{x}\rangle)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sigma_{max}^{2}H_{s_{0}}\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\|.

Now if
\bm{w}_{1}
and
\bm{w}_{2}
both belong to
\{\bm{w}:{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\leq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{0})\}
, we have
for any
\bm{x}_{i}\in\bm{S}
,
\langle\bm{w}_{1},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>\ell^{-1}(N{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{0}))
, and
\langle\bm{w}_{2},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>\ell^{-1}(N{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{0}))
. Following the same routine as the locally smooth proof, we complete the second argument.

The proof is completed.
∎

Based on Assumption
2
, we also have the following lemma characterizing the relationship between loss
\ell
and its derivative
\ell^{\prime}
when
x
is large enough.

Lemma 7
.

Let loss
\ell
satisfy Assumption
2
. Then, there exists an large enough
x_{0}
and a positive real
K
, such that,
\forall x>x_{0}
, we have

-\frac{1}{4}\ell^{\prime}(x)\leq\ell(x)\leq-4\ell^{\prime}(x).

Proof.

By Assumption
2
, there exists a large enough
x_{0}
, such that
\forall x>x_{0}
, we have

\frac{1}{2}e^{-x}\leq-\ell^{\prime}(x)\leq 2e^{-x}.

(11)

On the other hand, as
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\ell(x)=0
, we have

\displaystyle\ell(x)=\int_{s=x}^{\infty}-\ell^{\prime}(s)\mathrm{d}s,

which by Eq. (
11
) leads to

\frac{1}{2}e^{-x}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-s}\mathrm{d}s\leq\ell(x)\leq 2\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-s}\mathrm{d}s=2e^{-x}.

The proof is completed.
∎

By Lemma
7
, we immediately get the following corollary:

Corollary 2
.

Let loss
\ell
satisfy Assumption
2
. Then, there exist positive reals
C_{g}
and
C_{l}
, such that, for any
\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying either
\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|\leq C_{g}
or
{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\leq C_{l}
, we have

\frac{\gamma}{4}{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\leq\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|\leq 4{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}).

Proof.

We start with the case
\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|\leq C_{g}
. By simple calculation, we have

\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|=\frac{1}{N}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}\right\|\leq-\frac{\sigma_{max}}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle),

(12)

and

\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|\geq\langle\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}),\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle\geq-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle).

(13)

By Assumption
2
, we have there exists a constant
C_{g}^{\prime}
, s.t., any
x
with
-\ell^{\prime}(x)>C_{g}^{\prime}
satisfies
x>x_{0}
. Let
C_{g}=\frac{C_{g}^{\prime}\gamma}{N}
. We then have if
\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|\leq C_{g}
, then
\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>x_{0}
(
\forall i
), and thus
4\ell(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\geq-\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\geq\frac{1}{4}\ell(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)
. Combing Eqs. (
12
) and (
13
), we then have

4{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})=4\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\geq\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|\geq\frac{\gamma}{4N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)=\frac{\gamma}{4}{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}).

Similarly, as for the case
{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\leq C_{l}
, we have there exists a constant
C_{l}^{\prime}
, s.t., any
x
with
\ell(x)<C_{l}^{\prime}
satisfies
x>x_{0}
. Let
C_{l}=\frac{C_{l}\sigma_{max}}{N}
and the rest of the proof follows the same routine as the first case.

The proof is completed.
∎

The following lemma bridges the second moment of
\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(t)}
with its squared first moment.

Lemma 8
.

Let the dataset
\bm{S}
satisfies the separable assumption
1
. Let
\bm{B}
be a random subset of
\bm{S}
with size
b
sampled independently and uniformly without replacement. Then, at any point
\bm{w}
, we have

\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}_{\bm{B}}\left[\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}}(\bm{w})\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{N\sigma_{max}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}b}\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|^{2}.

Proof.

To start with, notice that

\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|=\|{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{B}}\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}}(\bm{w})\|\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{B}}\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}}(\bm{w})\|.

Therefore, the first inequality can be directly obtained by Cauchy-Schwartz’s inequality. To prove the second inequality, we first calculate the explicit form of
\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}}(\bm{w})
.

\displaystyle\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}}(\bm{w})\|^{2}=\frac{1}{b^{2}}\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{B}}\nabla\tilde{\ell}(\bm{w},\bm{x})\right\|^{2}=\frac{1}{b^{2}}\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{B}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle)\bm{x}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{\sigma_{max}^{2}}{b^{2}}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{B}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle)^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{B}}\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}}(\bm{w})\|^{2}\leq\frac{\sigma_{max}^{2}}{Nb}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle)^{2}\leq\frac{\sigma_{max}^{2}}{Nb}\left(\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle)\right)^{2}.

(14)

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|=\frac{1}{N}\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle)\bm{x}\right\|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\left\langle\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle)\bm{x},-\frac{\hat{\bm{w}}}{\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|}\right\rangle\overset{(\star)}{\geq}\frac{\gamma}{N}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle)

where Eq. (
\star
) is due to
\forall\bm{x}\in\bm{S}
,
\langle\bm{x},-\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle\geq 1
and
\ell^{\prime}<0
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})\|^{2}\geq\frac{\gamma^{2}}{N^{2}}\left(\sum_{\bm{x}\in\bm{S}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}\rangle)\right)^{2}.

(15)

The proof is completed by putting Eqs. (
14
) and (
15
) together.
∎

In the following lemma, we show the updates of GDM, Adam, and SGDM are all non-zero.

Lemma 9
.

Regardless of GDM, Adam, or SGDM, the updates of all steps are non-zero, i.e.,

\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|>0,\forall t>1.

Proof.

We start with the alternative forms of the update rule of GDM, Adam, and SGDM using the gradients along the trajectory respectively. For GDM, by Eq. (
1
), the update rule can be written as

\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)=-\eta(1-\beta)\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s))\right).

(16)

Similarly, the update rule of SGDM can be written as

\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)=-\eta(1-\beta)\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{B}(s)}(\bm{w}(s))\right),

(17)

while the update rule of Adam can be given as

\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)=-\eta\frac{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1-\beta_{1}}{1-\beta_{1}^{s}}\beta_{1}^{t-s}\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s))}{\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathbf{1}_{d}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1-\beta_{2}}{1-\beta_{2}^{s}}\beta_{2}^{t-s}(\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s)))^{2}}}.

(18)

On the other hand, by the definition of empirical risk
{\mathcal{L}}
, the gradient of
{\mathcal{L}}
at point
\bm{w}
can be given as

\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w})=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}}{N}.

(19)

By Eq. (
19
) and Eq. (
16
), we further have for GDM,

\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)=-\eta(1-\beta)\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(s),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}}{N}\right).

(20)

By Assumption
1
, there exists a non-zero parameter
\hat{\bm{w}}
, such that,
\langle\hat{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0
,
\forall i
. Therefore, by executing inner product between Eq. (
20
) and
\hat{\bm{w}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|\geq\langle\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t),\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(1-\beta)\eta\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(s),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\langle\bm{x}_{i},\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle}{N}\right)\overset{(*)}{>}0,

where Eq.
(*)
is due to
\ell^{\prime}<0
. This complete the proof for GDM.

Similarly, for SGDM, we have

\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|\geq-\eta(1-\beta)\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\frac{\sum_{(\bm{x},\bm{y})\in\bm{B}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(s),\bm{y}\bm{x}\rangle)\langle\bm{y}\bm{x},\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle}{b}\right)>0,

which completes the proof of SGDM.

For Adam, we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|\left\|\hat{\bm{w}}\odot\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathbf{1}_{d}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1-\beta_{2}}{1-\beta_{2}^{s}}\beta_{2}^{t-s}(\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s)))^{2}}\right\|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-\left\langle\hat{\bm{w}}\odot\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathbf{1}_{d}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1-\beta_{2}}{1-\beta_{2}^{s}}\beta_{2}^{t-s}(\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s)))^{2}},\eta\frac{\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1-\beta_{1}}{1-\beta_{1}^{s}}\beta_{1}^{t-s}\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s))}{\sqrt{\varepsilon\mathbf{1}_{d}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1-\beta_{2}}{1-\beta_{2}^{s}}\beta_{2}^{t-s}(\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s)))^{2}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\hat{\bm{w}},\eta\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1-\beta_{1}}{1-\beta_{1}^{s}}\beta_{1}^{t-s}\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(s)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\eta\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1-\beta_{1}}{1-\beta_{1}^{s}}\beta_{1}^{t-s}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(s),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\langle\bm{x}_{i},\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle}{N}\right)>0,

which completes the proof of Adam.

The proof is completed.
∎

Appendix B
Implicit regularization of GD/SGD with momentum

This section collects the proof of the implicit regularization of gradient descent with momentum and stochastic gradient descent with momentum. The analyses of this section hold for almost every dataset, and the "almost every" constraint is further moved in Section
E.1
.

B.1
Implicit regularization of GD with Momentum

This section collects the proof of Theorem
2
.

B.1.1
Proof of the sum of squared gradients converges

To begin with, we will prove the sum of squared norm of gradients along the trajectory is finite for gradient descent with momentum. To see this, we first define
the continuous-time update rule as

\bm{w}(t+\alpha)-\bm{w}(t)=\alpha(\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)),\forall t\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},\forall\alpha\in[0,1].

We then prove a generalized case of Lemma
1
for any
\bm{w}(t+\alpha)
.

Lemma 10

(Lemma
1
, extended)
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
2
hold. We then have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))+\frac{\beta}{2\eta(1-\beta)}\|\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\|^{2}\geq

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t+\alpha))+\frac{\beta}{2\eta(1-\beta)}\alpha^{2}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(1-{C_{1}})\alpha^{2}}{\eta}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2},

(21)

where
{C_{1}}
is a positive real such that
\eta=2\frac{N}{H_{s_{0}}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{C_{1}}
and
s_{0}\overset{\triangle}{=}\ell^{-1}(N{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}_{1}))
.

Proof of Lemma
10
.

We prove this lemma by reduction to absurdity.

Concretely, let
t^{*}
be the smallest positive integer time such that there exists an
\alpha\in[0,1]
, such that Eq. (
21
) doesn’t hold. Let
\alpha^{*}=\inf\{\alpha\in[0,1]:Eq.~(\ref{eq: gdm_direct_taylor_extended})~doesn^{\prime}t~hold~for~(t^{*},\alpha)\}
. By continuity, Eq. (
21
) holds for
(t^{*},\alpha^{*})
.

We further divide the proof into two cases depending on the value of
\alpha^{*}
.

Case 1:
\alpha^{*}=0
: For any
t^{*}>t\geq 1
, we have Eq. (
21
) holds for
(t,1)
. Specifically, we have

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\|^{2}\geq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t+1))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2},

which further leads to

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))={\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(1)-\bm{w}(0)\|^{2}\geq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}.

Since
\frac{\beta}{2\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}
is non-negative, we have

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))\geq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*})).

By Lemma
6
, we have
{\mathcal{L}}
is
H_{s_{0}}
smooth at
\bm{w}(t^{*})
. Therefore, by Taylor’s expansion for
{\mathcal{L}}
at point
\bm{w}(t^{*})
, we have for small enough
\alpha>0

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\langle\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*})),\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\rangle+\frac{H_{s_{0}}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\alpha\langle\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*})),\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\rangle+\frac{H_{s_{0}}\alpha^{2}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{=}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\alpha\left\langle\frac{1}{(1-\beta)\eta}(\beta(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1))-(\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*}))),\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\right\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{H_{s_{0}}\alpha^{2}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\alpha\beta}{(1-\beta)\eta}\left\langle(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1),\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\right\rangle+\left(\frac{H_{s_{0}}\alpha^{2}\sigma_{max}}{2N}-\frac{\alpha}{(1-\beta)\eta}\right)\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(**)}{\leq}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|(\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\frac{H_{s_{0}}\alpha^{2}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}-\frac{\alpha}{(1-\beta)\eta}\right)\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}+\left(\frac{\alpha\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}-\frac{\alpha}{(1-\beta)\eta}+\frac{H_{s_{0}}\alpha^{2}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}\right)\|(\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+{\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}-\frac{(1-\alpha)\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}}

\displaystyle{+\left(\frac{\alpha\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}-\frac{\alpha}{(1-\beta)\eta}+\frac{H_{s_{0}}\alpha^{2}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}\right)\|(\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\diamond)}{\leq}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}-\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\alpha^{2}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-{C_{1}})\alpha^{2}}{\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2},

(22)

where Eq.
(*)
is due to a simple rearrangement of the update rule of gradient descent with momentum (Eq. (
1
)), i.e.,

\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))=\frac{1}{(1-\beta)\eta}(\beta(\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1))-(\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t))),\forall t\geq 1,

(23)

Inequality
(**)
is due to Cauchy Schwarz’s inequality and arithmetic-geometric average inequality, and Inequality
(\diamond)
is due to

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-\alpha)\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}+\left(\frac{\alpha\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}-\frac{\alpha}{(1-\beta)\eta}+\frac{H_{s_{0}}\alpha^{2}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}\right)\|(\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-\alpha)\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\alpha^{2}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}-\frac{(1-{C_{1}})\alpha^{2}}{\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}.

Here the inequality is due to that
-\frac{(1-\alpha)\beta}{2\eta}\|(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}
tend to
-\frac{\beta}{2\eta}\|(\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}
as
\alpha
tend to zero, which is a negative constant by Lemma
9
, and
-\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\alpha^{2}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}-\frac{(1-{C_{1}})\alpha^{2}}{\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}
is
\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})
.

Eq. (
22
) indicates Eq. (
21
) holds at
(t^{*},\alpha)
for
\alpha>0
is small enough, which contradicts to
\alpha^{*}=0
.

Case 2:
\alpha^{*}\neq 0
: Same as
Case 1
, we have for any
1\leq t<t^{*}
,

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\|^{2}\geq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t+1))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2},

which further leads to

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))\geq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\beta}{2\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}.

(24)

On the other hand, by the definition of
\alpha^{*}
, we have for any
0\leq\alpha<\alpha^{*}
, we have Eq. (
21
) holds for
(t^{*},\alpha)
, which by continuity further leads to Eq. (
21
) holds for
(t^{*},\alpha^{*})
. Therefore,
\alpha^{*}<1
, otherwise, Eq. (
21
) holds for
(t^{*},\alpha)
,
\forall\alpha\in[0,1]
which contradicts the definition of
t^{*}
.

Combining Eq. (
21
) with
(t^{*},\alpha)
and Eq. (
24
), we further have

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))\geq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\alpha^{2}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{1-{C_{1}}}{2{C_{1}}}\alpha^{2}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2},

Consequently, for any
\alpha\in[0,\alpha^{*}]

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))\geq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha)),

and by Lemma
6
, we then have
{\mathcal{L}}
is
\frac{H_{s_{0}}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{N}
smooth at
\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha)
, which further by Taylor’s expansion leads to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha^{*}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\langle\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*})),\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*})\rangle+\frac{H_{s_{0}}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+\alpha^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\circ)}{\leq}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\alpha^{*}\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha^{*}\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\frac{H_{s_{0}}(\alpha^{*})^{2}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}-\frac{\alpha^{*}}{(1-\beta)\eta}\right)\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\alpha^{*}\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}+\left(\frac{H_{s_{0}}(\alpha^{*})^{2}\sigma_{max}^{2}}{2N}-\frac{{\alpha^{*}}(2-\beta)}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\right)\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\bullet)}{=}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\alpha^{*}\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}+\left(\frac{{C_{1}}(\alpha^{*})^{2}}{\eta}-\frac{\alpha^{*}(2-\beta)}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\right)\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ast)}{<}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t^{*}))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*})-\bm{w}(t^{*}-1)\|^{2}-\frac{(\alpha^{*})^{2}\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{(1-{C_{1}})(\alpha^{*})^{2}}{\eta}\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}

where Eq. (
\circ
) follows the same routine as
Case 1
, Eq. (
\bullet
) is due to the definition of
\eta
and
{C_{1}}
, and Eq. (
\ast
) is due to
\alpha^{*}<1
, and
\|\bm{w}(t^{*}+1)-\bm{w}(t^{*})\|^{2}>0
(given by Lemma
9
).

By the continuity of
{\mathcal{L}}
, for any small enough
\delta>0
, Eq. (
21
) holds for
(t^{*},\alpha^{*}+\delta)
, which contradicts to the definition of
\alpha^{*}
.

The proof is completed.
∎

By Lemma
1
, one can easily obtain the sum of the squared norms of the updates across the trajectory converges.

Corollary 3
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
2
hold. We have

\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}<\infty.

(25)

Consequentially, we have

\|\bm{w}(t)\|=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{t}).

Proof.

By Lemma
1
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\|^{2}-

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t+1))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1-{C_{1}}}{\eta}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2},

which by summing over
t
further leads to

{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))\geq{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(1))-\left({\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t+1))+\frac{\beta}{2(1-\beta)\eta}\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}\right)\geq\frac{1-{C_{1}}}{\eta}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\|\bm{w}(s+1)-\bm{w}(s)\|^{2}.

Taking
t\rightarrow\infty
leads to

\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\|\bm{w}(s+1)-\bm{w}(s)\|^{2}<\infty.

By triangle inequality, we further have

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}(t)\|\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t}\|\bm{w}(s+1)-\bm{w}(s)\|+\|\bm{w}(1)\|

\displaystyle\overset{(\star)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\sqrt{t\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\|\bm{w}(s+1)-\bm{w}(s)\|^{2}\right)}+\|\bm{w}(1)\|=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{t}),

where Eq. (
\star
) is due to Cauchy-Schwartz’s inequality.

The proof is completed.
∎

By the negative derivative of the loss and the separable data, we can finally prove the sum of squared gradient converges.

Corollary 4
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
2
hold. We have,
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\|^{2}<\infty
.

Proof.

By Eq. (
20
), we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\left\|(1-\beta)\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(s),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}}{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta^{2}(1-\beta)^{2}\left\|\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(s),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}}{N}\right\|^{2}{\frac{\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|^{2}}{\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|^{2}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\geq}

\displaystyle\eta^{2}{\gamma^{2}}(1-\beta)\left\langle\hat{\bm{w}},\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(s),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}}{N}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(**)}{\geq}

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\gamma^{2}(1-\beta)^{2}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\beta^{t-s}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(s),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)}{N}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\gamma^{2}(1-\beta)^{2}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)}{N}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\bullet)}{\geq}

\displaystyle\frac{\eta^{2}\gamma^{2}(1-\beta)^{2}}{\sigma_{max}^{2}}\left\|\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}}{N}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\eta^{2}\gamma^{2}(1-\beta)^{2}}{\sigma_{max}^{2}}\left\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\right\|^{2},

(26)

where Inequality
(*)
is due to Cauchy-Schwartz’s inequality, Inequality
(**)
is due to
\ell^{\prime}(s)<0
,
\forall s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\langle\hat{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle\geq\gamma
,
\forall i\in[N]
, and Inequality (
\bullet
) is due to the definition of
\sigma_{max}
. By combining Eq. (
25
) and Eq. (
26
), we complete the proof.
∎

By the exponential-tailed assumption of the loss (Assumption
2
), we further have the following corollary.

Corollary 5
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
2
hold. Then,
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\right\|=0
, and

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle=\infty,\forall i.

Consequently, there exists an large enough time
t_{0}
, such that,
\forall t>t_{0}
,
\forall i
, we have
\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0
, and

\displaystyle-\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\leq(1+e^{-\mu_{+}\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle})e^{-\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle},

\displaystyle-\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\geq(1-e^{-\mu_{-}\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle})e^{-\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}.

B.1.2
Parameter dynamics

To prove Theorem
2
, we only need to show
\bm{w}(t)-\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}

(t\geq 1)
has bounded norm for any iteration
t>0
. Letting
{C_{3}}=\frac{\eta}{N}
in Corollary
1
, we obtain an constant vector
\tilde{\bm{w}}
satisfying Eq. (
10
). Define

\bm{r}(t)\overset{\triangle}{=}\bm{w}(t)-\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}-\tilde{\bm{w}}.

(27)

As
\tilde{\bm{w}}
is a constant vector, that
\bm{w}(t)-\ln(t)\hat{\bm{w}}

(t\geq 1)
has bounded norm is equivalent to
\bm{r}(t)
has bounded norm. As discussed in the main body of the paper, we then propose an equivalent proposition of
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
is bounded, and further prove this proposition is fulfilled. Specifically, we have

Lemma 11
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
2
hold. Then,
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
is bounded if and only if the function
g(t)
is upper bounded, where
g:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\displaystyle g(t)\overset{\triangle}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\rangle-\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\langle\bm{r}(\tau)-\bm{r}(\tau-1),\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\rangle.

(28)

Furthermore, for almost every dataset, we have
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}(g(t+1)-g(t))
is upper bounded.

As the proof is rather complex, we separate it into two sub-lemmas. We first prove
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
is bounded if and only if function
g(t)
is upper bounded.

Lemma 12

(First argument in Lemma
11
)
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
2
hold. Then,
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
is bounded if and only if function
g(t)
is upper bounded.

Proof.

We start the proof by showing that
A_{1}(t)\overset{\triangle}{=}\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\langle\bm{r}(\tau)-\bm{r}(\tau-1),\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\rangle
has bounded absolute value.

By the definition of
\bm{r}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{r}(t)-\bm{r}(t-1)=

\displaystyle\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)-\ln\left(\frac{t}{t-1}\right)\hat{\bm{w}},

which further indicates

A_{1}(t)=\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left\langle\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)-\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau-1}\right)\hat{\bm{w}},\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\right\rangle.

Therefore, the absolute value of
A_{1}(t)
can be bounded as

\displaystyle|A_{1}(t)|=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left\langle\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)-\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau-1}\right)\hat{\bm{w}},\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left|\left\langle\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)-\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau-1}\right)\hat{\bm{w}},\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left\|\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\right\|^{2}+\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left|\left\langle\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau-1}\right)\hat{\bm{w}},\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left\|\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\right\|^{2}+\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left\|\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau-1}\right)\hat{\bm{w}}\right\|\left\|\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\right\|

\displaystyle\overset{(\star)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left\|\bm{w}(\tau)-\bm{w}(\tau-1)\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\tau=2}^{t}\left\|\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau-1}\right)\hat{\bm{w}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(\circ)}{<}

\displaystyle\infty,

where Inequality
(\star)
is due to the Inequality of arithmetic and geometric means, and Inequality
(\circ)
is due to Corollary
3
and
\ln\frac{\tau}{\tau-1}={\bm{O}}(\frac{1}{\tau})
.

Therefore,
g(t)
is upper bounded is then equivalent to
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\rangle
is upper bounded. Now if
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\rangle
is upper bounded, we will prove
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
is bounded by reduction to absurdity.

Suppose that
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
has unbounded norm. By Corollary
3
, we have
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\|=0
, and there exists a large enough time
T
, such that
\|\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\|<1
for any
t\geq T
. On the other hand, since
\bm{r}(t)
is unbounded from above, there exists an increasing time sequence
k_{i}>T
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, such that

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\|\bm{r}(k_{i})\|=\infty.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\varliminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(k_{i})\|^{2}+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(k_{i}),\bm{w}(k_{i})-\bm{w}(k_{i}-1)\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\varliminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(k_{i})\|^{2}-\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\left\|\bm{r}(k_{i})\right\|\left\|\bm{w}(k_{i})-\bm{w}(k_{i}-1)\right\|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\varliminf_{i\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(k_{i})\|^{2}-\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\left\|\bm{r}(k_{i})\right\|=\infty,

which leads to contradictory, and completes the proof of necessity.

On the other hand, if
\|\bm{r}(t)\|
is upper bounded, since
\|\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\|
is also upper bounded, we have
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\rangle
is upper bounded, which completes the proof of sufficiency.

The proof is completed.
∎

Therefore, the last piece of this puzzle is to prove
g(t)
is upper bounded
\forall t>0
.

Lemma 13

(Second argument in Lemma
11
)
.

Let all conditions in Theorem
2
hold. Then, for almost every dataset, we have that
g(t)
is upper bounded.

Proof.

We start the proof by calculating
g(t+1)-g(t)
. For any
t\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle g(t+1)-g(t)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t)\rangle+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t+1),\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{w}(t)-\bm{w}(t-1)\rangle-\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t),\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t)\rangle+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{w}(t+1)+\bm{w}(t-1)-2\bm{w}(t)\rangle.

On the other hand, by simply rearranging the update rule Eq. (
1
), we have

\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}(\bm{w}(t+1)+\bm{w}(t-1)-2\bm{w}(t))=-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))-(\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)),

(29)

which further indicates

\displaystyle g(t+1)-g(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t)\rangle+\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))-(\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}+\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\ln\left(\frac{t+1}{t}\right)\hat{\bm{w}}-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\right\rangle.

Denote
A_{2}(t)=\|\bm{r}(t+1)-\bm{r}(t)\|^{2}
, and
A_{3}(t)=\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\ln\left(\frac{t+1}{t}\right)\hat{\bm{w}}-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\right\rangle
. We then prove respectively
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}A_{2}(t)
and
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}A_{3}(t)
are upper bounded.

First of all, by definition of
\bm{r}(t)
Eq.(
27
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}A_{2}(t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\left(\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}+\ln\left(\frac{t+1}{t}\right)^{2}\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|^{2}-2\ln\left(\frac{t+1}{t}\right)\langle\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t),\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\left(\|\bm{w}(t+1)-\bm{w}(t)\|^{2}+\ln\left(\frac{t+1}{t}\right)^{2}\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|^{2}\right)\overset{(\bullet)}{<}\infty,

(30)

where Eq. (
\bullet
) is due to Lemma
3
and
\ln\left(\frac{t+1}{t}\right)=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{t})
.

Then we only need to prove
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}A_{3}(t)<\infty
.

To begin with, by adding one additional term
\frac{1}{t}\hat{\bm{w}}
into
A_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{3}(t)=

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\frac{1}{t}\hat{\bm{w}}-\ln\left(\frac{t+1}{t}\right)\hat{\bm{w}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\frac{1}{t}\hat{\bm{w}}-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\right\rangle.

On the one hand, by Corollary
3
,
\|\bm{w}(t)\|=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{t})
, which further leads to

\|\bm{r}(t)\|=\|\bm{w}(t)\|+\ln(t)\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|+\|\hat{\bm{w}}\|=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{t})

By
\frac{1}{t}-\ln\frac{t+1}{t}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t^{2}}\right)
, we have

\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\frac{1}{t}\hat{\bm{w}}-\ln\left(\frac{t+1}{t}\right)\hat{\bm{w}}\right\rangle=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}}}\right).

(31)

On the other hand, by direct calculation of the gradient, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\frac{1}{t}\hat{\bm{w}}-\eta\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{w}(t))\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\frac{1}{t}\hat{\bm{w}}-\frac{\eta}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{(\star)}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\frac{1}{t}\eta\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\in\bm{S}_{s}}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\bm{x}_{i}-\eta\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\eta\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\in\bm{S}_{s}}\left(\frac{1}{t}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}+\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\right)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle-{\frac{1}{N}}\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\eta\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle,

where Eq. (
\star
) is due to the definition of
\tilde{\bm{w}}
(Eq. (
10
) with
{C_{3}}=\eta/N
).

Denote

A_{4}(t)=-\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\eta\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s}}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle,

and

A_{5}(t)=\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\eta\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\in\bm{S}_{s}}\left(\frac{1}{t}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}+\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\right)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle.

We then analysis these two terms respectively. As for
A_{4}(t)
, due to
\ell^{\prime}<0
, we have

\displaystyle A_{4}(t)\leq-\eta\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s},\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle.

By Corollary
5
, we further have
\forall t>t_{0}

-\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\leq(1+e^{-\mu_{+}\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle})e^{-\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\leq 2e^{-\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle},

which further indicates

\displaystyle A_{4}(t)\leq

\displaystyle-\eta\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s},\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0}\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\eta\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s},\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0}2e^{-\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s},\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0}2e^{-\langle\bm{r}(t)+\ln t\hat{\bm{w}}+\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\eta\left(\max_{i}e^{\langle-\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\right)\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s},\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0}2e^{-\langle\bm{r}(t)+\ln t\hat{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{(\circ)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\eta\frac{\left(\max_{i}e^{\langle-\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\right)}{t^{\theta}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s},\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle>0}2e^{-\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{(\diamond)}{\leq}

\displaystyle\eta\frac{\left(\max_{i}e^{\langle-\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\right)}{t^{\theta}}2N,

where
\theta
in Eq.
(\circ)
is defined as

\theta=\min_{\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s}}\langle\bm{x}_{i},\hat{\bm{w}}\rangle>1.

(32)

As
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{t^{\theta}}<\infty
, we have

\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}A_{4}(t)<\infty.

(33)

For each term
\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\eta\left(\frac{1}{t}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}+\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\right)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle
(
\bm{x}_{i}\notin\bm{S}_{s}
) in
A_{5}(t)
, we divide the analysis into two parts depending on the sign of
\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle
.

Case 1:
\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle\geq 0
.
By Corollary
5
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\eta\left(\frac{1}{t}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}+\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\right)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\eta\left(\frac{1}{t}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}+\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\right)\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\eta\left(-\frac{1}{t}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}+(1+e^{-\mu_{+}\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle})e^{-\langle\bm{w}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\right)\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{(\diamond)}{=}

\displaystyle\eta\left(-\frac{1}{t}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}+(1+e^{-\mu_{+}\langle\bm{r}(t)+\ln t\hat{\bm{w}}+\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle})e^{-\langle\bm{r}(t)+\ln t\hat{\bm{w}}+\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}\right)\left\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle,

where Eq. (
\diamond
) is due to the definition of
\bm{r}(t)
(Eq. (
27
).

Since
\langle\bm{r}(t),\bm{x}_{i}\rangle\geq 0
, we further have

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{r}(t),-\eta\left(\frac{1}{t}e^{-\langle\tilde{\bm{w}},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle}+\ell^{\prime}(\langle\bm{w},\bm{x}_{i}\rangle)\right)\bm{x}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq
